ParlaMint-AT_1996-05-23-020-XX-NRSITZ-00025_d7e12230	Mr President. High house! My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! The urgent question by Members of the Liberal Forum gives me an opportunity to comment on a number of fundamental comments on media policy. Just a few days ago, at the celebration of the 50th anniversary of the Austrian Magazine Association, I welcomed the intensification of the media-political discussion in Austria and took part in it. I have invited everyone in the media sector to participate in this discussion – the publishers, the journalists, the radio and television makers, the creatives, the advertisers and many others who feel called to do so. However, I would add today that we should not have this media debate for the sake of discussion, but rather for media policy to bring together everything that can be used in the forthcoming media policy decisions. In order to achieve this objective, however, the media debate must meet a number of conditions and requirements. In my eyes, it must first and foremost be targeted. For example, the discussion about a future media people's desire does not yet reveal what its real objective is. We do not know the text only in approaches, nor can we expect it soon, because, as we know from media, the popular desire should not be held until autumn. On the other hand, I would like to tell you here and today what my goal is in this debate and, above all, in the decisions we are about to take. In view of the rapid technological development and accelerated internationalisation and globalisation, I would like to see there continue to be competitive Austrian media in the media sector. Especially a small country as part of a large language area with strong media groups must not lose sight of this objective. And that's what we're going to work on in media policy. I would also like this discussion to be conducted in a comprehensive and complete manner. I mean that it is not a matter of practicing punctual criticism or showing solutions, but rather of discussing the challenges and possible solutions in their entirety. I do not think it would be useful to criticise a newspaper at national level because of its market power without, for example, saying that it is at regional level. Level leaves give, whose market power is much greater. There will also be no meaningful media discussion if we concentrate only on the ORF or on the printing sector, without simultaneously addressing the wide range of interrelationships between the electronic and print sectors. I would also like this media debate to be conducted honestly. I am not addressing the moral category of honesty or untruth, but the intellectual honesty and willingness to discuss the real conditions for media making in Austria, its problems and prospects. I would like to explain to two examples what I mean by this: One of our structural deficits is the fact that we in Austria are less recruited per capita of the population, which is still less recruited than in comparable other countries in Europe. At the same time, however, we have an advertising charge that prevents more money from flowing into the media or that more money can be advertised for the same money. This honesty, however, also includes discussing not only the amount and distribution of 275 million schillings currently spent for press and publicity promotion, but also very openly saying that the preferential transport tariff for newspapers, magazines and other printing works costs the Austrian Post only 2 billion schillings in one year according to their own data – and thus, of course, brings the publishers, the publishers of these printing works. Finally, it would be good – and I will in all cases make every effort from my side – to keep this discussion open. I mean that we are clearly expressing the interests that are behind some kind of media policy proposal. Thus, it makes no sense to brand the planned conversion of the ORF into an economically comprehensive manageable AG as a politicalisation, without at the same time adding that by reducing the future Supervisory Board against today's board of trustees would naturally increase the weight of the supervisors sent by the parliamentary opposition. And it also makes no sense to attach to a media-political discussion if your own publishing success is not desirable. It is equally open to say who should cover the operations of individual newspapers, if not the respective owners. Ladies and gentlemen! I am therefore speaking out for a targeted, comprehensive, honest and open media discussion in Austria. That is what I meant when I said a few days ago that this debate must not be a taboo. I do not think it a goal to seek salvation in a general media discussion in order to safeguard the interests of one's own medium, to safeguard the interests of one's own sector. It has no meaning, but it is said to be counterproductive to play the individual media and the individual sectors against each other. I would also like, Mr Fischenschlager, not to hide that you have offered me a premiere today by talking here in the House about individual companies and individual companies, about the internal market of individual companies, advertising concepts et cetera, because I believe that this will not contribute to a constructive media discussion. In addition, in view of the real major challenges facing the media, cooperation and not the opposite is the only way to ensure that there are not only viable, but also powerful Austrian media in the future. I now come to answer your questions and send them in advance, Mr President: It cannot be in the nature of the technical treatment of an urgent request, in the short time in which one can read the questions and work out their answers to cover everything. In other words, if you are still missing or disappearing in my comprehensive answers, I ask you to understand that the debate is not over today and that you can then talk about it even further in later stages. Question 1: The government has by no means failed to develop appropriate government proposals, but it is – on the contrary – working out them. I have already pointed out at the constituent meeting of the new ORF Curatorium at the beginning of May, at which one of the applicants was present, that I am optimistic that a political agreement can be reached by the Regional Radio and Cable Act by the summer of this year, so that a government template can be sent for review during the summer. I believe that a media agreement in this area should provide sufficient guidance for cable operators and private programme providers on future investment. On question 2: In particular, the concentration processes in the media discussed in the request have moved the Austrian legislator, that is, this House, in 1993 to change the antitrust law. Since then, the antitrust law has included provisions on preventive merger control and a special scheme for media companies. In addition, the antitrust law also lays down media specific provisions in the framework of the abuse supervision of dominant companies. The amendments to the antitrust law adopted in 1993 and in particular the new merger control arrangements are applicable only to mergers carried out or will be implemented after the relevant antitrust legislation has entered into force. The legislator apparently assumed that, in the sense of the relevant jurisdiction of the Constitutional Court, it is necessary to protect the trust of companies in the continuation of a regulation which has been in force for years or decades. In addition to these constitutional considerations, the question should also be answered as to how to achieve retroactive unbundling at all. In return, the legislator has therefore decided to strengthen the supervision of abuse and has adopted a regulation relating in particular to media undertakings. Consequently, the cartel court may also apply measures to a dominant media company, inter alia, by which the dominant position is weakened or eliminated. Question 3: I cannot imagine a percentage ceiling for the printed or widespread edition of a periodic writing in a free society like ours. I am surprised that just from the camp of representatives of the Liberal Ideas seem to come, or come, that people want to prescribe what they should read or buy. This question seems to be overlooked in itself that anti-trust rules or anti-trust regulations cannot at all limit the internal growth of a company and the reduction of larger market shares. This is not possible at all due to the technique of anti-trust regulations. Anti-trust rules can prevent mergers, they can prevent mergers, mergers and so on, but they cannot and should not prevent any company – whether it is a newspaper or someone else – from reaching a larger audience on its own initiative. Media are now once more sensitive products than detergents. Helmut Schelsky, the well-known German sociologist, has therefore said a bit pointedly, but very correctly: Information has become the decisive means of production of society, and the monopoly of this means of production is the most promising form of political enforcement. There has been so far, but there has been a bureaucratic spruten run on district administration authorities, with opinions from the headmasters, with opinions from class teachers, with opinions from mayors and with opinions from school supervisors. And until these bureaucratic obstacles were taken, in many cases it was no longer necessary for individual events to rent this school room, because then this event was already over. I therefore welcome the fact that, in future, in cooperation with the teachers, the school holders can unbureaucratically rent out these premises.
